A.43 DICTO Kyushindo Cores (DICTO DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.23532013)

Told in Volume I, Chapter 4 · Cycles.

Theorem 1 (general). For a convex polytope S with O inside: maxv |v|²/hP(v) ≤ tmin(S) ≤ R(S)/r(P(S)) ≤ (R(S)/r(S))². Equality for the Platonic solids (9; 3, 3; 15 − 6√5, 15 − 6√5). Thin square bipyramids: tmin → ∞.

Theorem 2 (main). Every convex polyhedron with icosahedral rotational symmetry satisfies S ⊂ (15 − 6√5)·P(S) ⊂ φ·P(S); 15 − 6√5 ≈ 1.583592 < φ (841 < 845); sharp. Proof: r(S) ≥ κR(S), κ = √((5 + 2√5)/15) ≈ 0.794654; covering radius θ* = arccos κ ≈ 37.377°; the (2, 3, 5) Möbius triangle has sides 20.905°, 31.717°, 37.377°; the kite has diagonals θ* and 36° (sin 20.905° sin 31.717°/sin 37.377° = sin 18°).

  • Corollary 1: Sk = φk·Dk, nested for every k; Dk ⊂ Dk−1 only for k = 1, 2, 4 among k ≤ 9. Margins tmin(Dk), k = 0 … 8: 1.134, 1.117, 1.103, 1.146, 1.042, 1.087, 1.026, 1.060, 1.138 (all ≤ 3/φ² ≈ 1.1459, equality at k = 3; proved for every k ≤ 40 in Theorem 5; for every k, Conjecture 1).
  • Theorem 3: reciprocation about radius² 15 − 5√5 = cφ² (c = 10/φ⁴) maps beat k to beat 5 − k. Lemma 3: polar(P(S)) = T(polar S).
  • Cleo Towers: boundaries at beats 0 | 1 | 2 | 4 | 7 | 12; 32, 30, 32, 32, 60 towers (186); outer ID c³ ≈ 3.11 times the core. Polar Cleo Towers: 5 | 4 | 3 | 1 | −2 | −7; core RT/c² (30 rays); 30, 32, 30, 60, 32 towers (184).
  • OG (frame of #37): volume (−1000 + 1240√5)/1539. Hex Aktis (−2 + 2√5)/81 ≈ 0.030520, height √((6 + 2√5)/27) ≈ 0.622782, 20 take ≈ 52.99%; Deltoid Aktis (−4 + 8√5)/1539 ≈ 0.009024, height √((42 + 2√5)/109) ≈ 0.652955, 60 take ≈ 47.01%.
  • OTT: 38 rays; Oktaktis similar to the Cleo-Icos* (height²/edge² = φ⁴/12, side²/edge² = (φ² + 1)/4); OG hexagon circumradius² = triangle inradius² = (6 − 2√5)/27.
  • OG turned out: towers 80, 132, 140 in 2, 3, 3 shapes; outer radius grows 1.52, 1.53, 1.61, 1.51 per beat; full flip (float) about 1.9 per beat. Shell 4: 200 towers (≈ 570.644662, 889.860165, 147.140716, 5699.759901), shell ≈ 210453.9306. Polar: 80, 132, 162 towers in 2, 3, 4 shapes.
  • TQH ribbons: trapezoid parallel sides² (38367 + 17019√5)/13448 and (6615 + 2925√5)/3362 (≈ 5.683 and 3.913, so the sides are ≈ 2.3839 and 1.9781), legs ≈ 0.4343, angles ≈ 62.15° and 117.85°, height ≈ 0.3840, bent ≈ 23.28° (float). Ribbons return at down 8 and down 9. Down 5 to down 11: TQP-252, TQ-320, TQP-402, TQ-500, TQD-732, TQHD-662, TQP-672.

Sources. Smarandache; Kepler; Mackay; Takakura et al.; Bergman; Kramer; Hypsicles; Caspar and Klug; Kroto et al. (C60); Iijima (nanotubes); Kenshiro Abbe. Searched: 77 library sources with a text layer, 21 October 2026. Scripts: studies 2026-10-20-cleo-towers, -og-spires, -ott-rays, 2026-10-21-og-turned-out, 2026-10-21-nesting-proof (commit c3c8ff7).

* The icosahedron face pyramid (classical; Mackay, 1962). Rediscovered by DICTO as the Cleo-Icos.

A.K Kaleidohedra by DICTO (DICTO DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.23173809)

Told in Volume I, Findings and apps.

  • F1: edge directions meet at 60° three times and 72° three times; volume 2·φ/2 + 2·½ = φ²; tiles the sheared FCC lattice (DICTO FCC). Family known (Fedorov; Lalvani US 5,623,790 and US 4,723,382; Grünbaum 2010 classifies only rhombic monohedra); this member not found.
  • F5: known (Grünbaum 2010, Fig. 2(b)); DICTO's route, the Bain stretch, new (2026-10-01).
  • F10 (EKP cell, cube edge 2): 12 rhombi of edge 2/φ, long diagonal 2, angles 72° and 108°; 48 walls (24 with sides √2/φ, 2/φ, √2; 24 with √2/φ², 2/φ, √2); volume exactly 12 (each roof keeps 1/φ of itself); walls 22.24° off every icosahedral two-fold axis (165 edges checked).
  • F11: for every s > 0, 8 regular pentagons, 4 hexagons (108°, 108°, 108°, 108°, 144°, 144°), 2 rectangles; squares at s = 2/φ; volume = dodecahedron + s × 7.236068.
  • F12: Dogstar*, edges 2/φ⁴, 2/φ³, 2/φ², 2/φ; volume 1.527864 (cube edge 2), φ/2 at dodecahedron edge 1.
  • F15: tetrahedral cluster 228 faces (192 triangles, 36 rhombi), volume 4 Jewels; octahedral cluster round a sealed stella. F16: the same for dodecahedra and Dogstars.

Checks: verify-kaleido.mjs and verify-roof-fold.mjs (krp-core), verify-zome-parallelohedra (Polyhedraverse), verify-dicto-fcc (Rhombiverse), on every push.

* Dodecahedron stellation 8 (Hart, 1996). Rediscovered by DICTO as the Dogstar.

A.D DICTO Solids: Exact Geometry (DICTO DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.23606967)

Told in Volume I, Findings and apps.

  • Version 2 (24 October 2026): 29 solids; coordinates exact in ℚ(√5), or ℚ(∛2) for Samson and the regular octahedron at the Delian bond* (classical), and to 15 decimals; faces consistently wound; f-vector, exact volume, squared edge lengths, symmetry group, stated values; JSON and OFF; a copy scaled to volume 1.
  • Packings: 6 that fill space (three lattice packings of DICTO's skewed solids; the golden, keyed and Delian bonds) and 4 that do not, with exact lattice vectors and placements.
  • Inventory index.csv: 122 rows; established catalogue numbers (Johnson, uniform, Wenninger, Hart, Mosseri–Sadoc) and DICTO's D1–D75.
  • check.py (Python 3 + sympy): 712 checks, all passing, including the keyed solids as their convex hulls minus or plus 8 Tricaps.

* Named Delilah in DICTO's books.

The DICTOnary · four dimensions

Four-dimensional shapes

The four-dimensional shapes of the work, each shown by a figure from its paper: first DICTO's, then the known ones. They carry no D-number, which is kept for single solids and pieces in three dimensions. Credits as in the references at the end of the known shapes. Each is also read as a Vacuus (#87, widened in #110): the plates through the centre spanned by its two-dimensional faces. DICTO named three kinds: heart, when every plate carries a face and its opposite (exactly the centrally symmetric shapes); inherited heart, when some plates carry two or more faces without central symmetry; non-heart, when each plate carries one face.

DICTO's four-dimensional shapes

Schlegel view through one golden truncated tetrahedron, and the projection on the five-fold plane (from #109, Figure 4).
— Golden Truncated 5-cell
four-dimensional solid · #109
not in Volume I
the hull of the 20 permutations of (φ², φ², φ², φ, 0): 20 corners, 40 edges, 30 faces, 10 cells (5 regular tetrahedra and 5 golden truncated tetrahedra, hexagon sides 1 : φ); two edge lengths, √2 and φ√2. One of the five S5-orbits of the 600-cell's corners, given with its W(A4) Dynkin labels by Koca, Koç, Al-Barwani and Al-Farsi (2006) and Koca, Ozdes Koca and Al-Ajmi (2012); its cells, edges and face counts, its silences and its reading as a Vacuus are worked out in #109 and #110; the name is DICTO's. A Vacuus of the non-heart kind: its 30 faces lie on 30 plates, one on each, all mirrors of the 600-cell (#110, Theorem A)
Schlegel view through one golden cuboctahedron, and the projection on the five-fold plane (from #109, Figure 5).
— Golden Cantellated 5-cell
four-dimensional solid · #109
not in Volume I
the hull of the 30 permutations of (φ², φ², 1, 1, 0): 30 corners, 90 edges, 80 faces, 20 cells. Another of the five S5-orbits of the 600-cell's corners, credited as for the Golden Truncated 5-cell; worked out in #109 and #110; the name is DICTO's. A Vacuus of the non-heart kind: its 80 faces lie on 80 plates, one on each, 40 on mirrors and 40 on cross walls of the 600-cell (#110, Theorem A)
The three four-dimensional sweeps on their parameter lines, the All-Golden Polytopes marked (from #91, Figure 3).
— DICTO All-Golden Polytopes
four-dimensional solids (a family) · #91
not in Volume I
four-dimensional polytopes all of whose cells are golden cuts. The tesseract trimmed by its polar 16-cell has two: at μ = φ², 8 Icosaxes* and 16 Lesser Golden Cut Tetrahedra (D126); at μ = 2 + 1/φ², 8 Unborn Axes** and 16 Greater Golden Cut Tetrahedra (D125). In the 600-cell × 120-cell family the bitruncated stage gives two more, at μ = 2 − √5/2 and (19 + 9√5)/44, by a linear cell rule checked exactly at three points. Not found in the sources checked (#91)
* The truncated octahedron x = φ (Koca et al., 2015). DICTO's name: Icosaxes. ** The truncated octahedron x = 1/φ (Koca et al., 2015). DICTO's name: Unborn Axes.
Left, its corners and edges in the H4 Coxeter plane; right, its Vacuus cut in 3D through the centre, three sheets on mirrors drawn solid (drawn for this volume by the method of #110, Figures 2 and 7).
— Valley Charge
four-dimensional solid · #110
not in Volume I
Valley Charge, the golden station of the 600-cell (#110, Proposition 6; the name is DICTO's): the truncated 600-cell cut at μ = 1/2 + √5/5 on DICTO's Hopscotch line S(μ) = 600-cell ∩ μ·120-cell between the 600-cell and the 120-cell, each edge cut at 1/(1 + φ²) of its length from each end: 1440 corners, 720 cells, 2400 triangles and 1200 golden hexagons. Centrally symmetric, a Vacuus of the heart kind whose plates are exactly the 600-cell's 360 walls: 20 hexagons on each of the 60 mirrors, 8 triangles on each of the 300 cross walls (the central sections parallel to a pair of opposite cells) (#110, Proposition 6). Not found in the sources checked (#110)
The 120-cell's 720 pentagons in the H4 Coxeter plane, marked by a fewest cover of sixteen A* copies, one colour per symmetry group (from #110, Figure 7).
— the 120-cell's dual cats A*, B*, C3*
four-dimensional solids (three) · #110
not in Volume I
the polars of A, B and C3 about the common centre. A* has 10 corners, 30 edges, 40 triangles and 20 tetrahedral cells: the 5-cell with a low pyramid on each cell, its Kleetope (Grünbaum, 1967); B* has 20 corners, 80 edges, 90 faces and 30 cells, triangular bipyramids; C3* has 30 corners, 70 edges, 60 faces and 20 cells of the trigonal-trapezohedron type. For each of the 600-cell's 60 S5s, the 120-cell is exactly A* ∩ (−A)* ∩ C3* ∩ B* ∩ (−B)*. Every plate of every copy lies on one of the 120-cell's 60 mirrors: A* and B* are Vacuus of the inherited heart kind, C3* of the heart kind; the fins of the 120 A* copies, and those of the 120 B* copies, cover all 720 pentagons and all 1200 edges of the 120-cell (#110, Theorem B′). DICTO's hypothesis, confirmed in #110: the 120-cell's own cats are the duals of the 600-cell's. No names for the three duals were found in the sources checked (#110)

Known four-dimensional shapes in the work

Its four shadows, and the 48-corner section D85 with its equal-edged form (from #63).
{3,3,5} 600-cell* (Schläfli, 1852)
four-dimensional regular solid · #63
Volume I, Chapter 6
120 corners, 720 edges, 1200 triangles, 600 tetrahedra; #63 computes its sections exactly; its cell-first sections met in this volume are D85 and the three by Boole Stott (1900) in the known shapes. The 600-cell (Schläfli, 1852) is a Vacuus of the heart kind: its 1200 triangles lie on the 60 mirrors of its group, 20 on each (#107). In its four standard shadows 45, 16, 16 and 33 of its walls stay flat, and the outlines whose edge fins all lie in them are possible Vacuus, such as the icosahedron, dodecahedron and larger icosahedron of the corner-first shadow: DICTO's observation that the shadows carry interconnected lattices of possible Vacuus (#110)
* Named the White Queen in DICTO's books.
Its 120 dodecahedra layer by layer (from #65).
{5,3,3} 120-cell* (Schläfli, 1852)
four-dimensional regular solid · #65
Volume I, Chapter 6
600 corners, 1200 edges, 720 pentagons, 120 dodecahedra, in nine layers round one dodecahedron (1, 12, 20, 12, 30, 12, 20, 12, 1); #65 computes its exact atlas of sections; it holds ten inscribed 600-cells (#110). The 120-cell (Schläfli, 1852) is a Vacuus of the heart kind: its 720 pentagons lie on the same 60 mirrors, 12 on each (#107); its shadows keep the same walls, and in its cell-first shadow the dodecahedra and a truncated icosahedron at height ±(1/8 + φ/4) are possible Vacuus (#110)
* Named the Red Queen in DICTO's books.
The five S5-orbit hulls of the 600-cell; the expanded 5-cell in the middle (from #110, Figure 3).
— expanded 5-cell* (Elte, 1912; as an orbit of the 600-cell: Koca, Koç, Al-Barwani and Al-Farsi, 2006)
four-dimensional solid · #110
not in Volume I
the hull of the 20 roots of A4: 20 corners, 60 edges, 70 faces, 30 cells; with the Golden Truncated and Cantellated 5-cells and their mirror images, one of the five S5-orbits of the 600-cell's corners; the 120-cell holds 600 copies, hidden only in the walls of their own 600-cell (#110). The expanded 5-cell (Elte, 1912) is a Vacuus of the heart kind about the 600-cell's centre: its 70 faces lie on 5 cross walls, 14 on each, the central slices off the mirrors found in #108 (#110)
* DICTO's name: C3.
Its 30 cells, 10 permutohedra and 20 hexagonal prisms, pulled half apart round the empty centre (from #105).
— 4D permutohedron* (Schoute, 1911)
four-dimensional uniform solid · #105
not in Volume I
the hull of the 120 orders of 1, 2, 3, 4, 5: 120 corners, 240 edges, 150 faces (squares and hexagons), 30 cells (10 permutohedra and 20 hexagonal prisms); its copies fill four-space. The 4D permutohedron (Schoute, 1911) is a Vacuus of the heart kind: its 150 faces lie on exactly 65 central slices, of four kinds, each face on one (#105, Theorem 1). At the heart, five of the slices, those with the middle value held at 3, are DICTO 1:2 Truncated Octahedra (D177), crossing two by two in regular hexagons; the heart repeats in every odd dimension, and the slices number 3, 15, 65, 225, 770, … for 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, … values (#105, Theorems 2, 2b and 3). The 4D Vacuus is DICTO's; no earlier Vacuus in four dimensions was found (#105)
* The name permutohedron: Guilbaud and Rosenstiehl, 1963.

Vacuus of the shapes round the Queens

#110 reads the shapes built on the corners of the 600-cell and the 120-cell as Vacuus, about their common centre. The shapes are their finders'; the Vacuus readings are DICTO's. A shape is hidden in the 600-cell or the 120-cell when every plate lies on one of its walls: the 60 mirrors and the 300 cross walls of the 600-cell (the central sections parallel to a pair of opposite cells). The 600-cell, the 120-cell, A, B, C3, Valley Charge and the dual cats are in the entries above.

About the author

DICTO is the artist name of (Alan) J. Baker, an artist in Osaka, Japan. His background is architectural sculpture, stonecarving and Buddhist statuary. He trained as an architectural stonecarver at the City and Guilds of London Art School (1998–2001), worked at St James's Palace, and recarved the Roman lettering of the Civil Service Rifles War Memorial at Somerset House, London, for its rededication in 2002. Since 2009 he has worked in Japan in the sculpture and restoration of Buddhist statues, among them a statue at Takaosan Jingo-ji, Kyoto, traditionally attributed to Kōbō Daishi Kūkai.

He builds geometric sculpture from recycled PET bottles with two modular systems of his own, B1NR and DICTOspheres, joined without glue. He holds six Japanese design registrations; five rest on spherical projections of the Platonic solids, the five forms behind his name: Dodecahedron, Icosahedron, Cube, Tetrahedron, Octahedron. His sculptures have been shown in Kyoto at the Kyoto Municipal Museum of Art (Interdependent Origination III, 2025), the Insho Domoto Museum of Fine Arts, the Kyoto Botanical Garden, the Kansho Exhibition and the Kyoto Takasegawa Art Festival.

The geometry behind the sculptures led to ninety-eight papers, gathered in DICTO: Collected Geometric Works, and to seven books: Quanta of Space: A Manifesto and its second volume, The DICTOnary of Atoms: Quanta of Space II (this one), HexOct: The Hexagonality Law, Alicia in Wonderland: The Red & White Queens, The Golden Cycle, Star Cores: Spikes, Halos and Cycles and DICTONIC Bonds: from the Octet to the Rule of Halves (all 2026). Every paper and book is published openly with its own DICTO DOI at Zenodo. More at https://dictospheres.com.

The Five Orders of Space

One work by DICTO in eleven books and twelve volumes, in five rings

The work is built after two models, credited as models. From Miyamoto Musashi’s Go Rin no Sho, The Book of Five Rings (五輪書, about 1645), it takes its five rings, Earth, Water, Fire, Wind and the Void. In paraphrase, not quoted from any translation: for Musashi, Earth lays out the way, Water is form and technique, Fire is the encounter, Wind is the knowledge of other schools, and the Void is what lies beyond. From Euclid’s Elements (about 300 BC) it takes its build: the Definitions are the DICTOnary (Book One, II: The DICTOnary of Atoms), the Postulates are Quanta of Space I (Book One, I: the manifesto), and the Propositions, with their proofs, are Books Two to Eleven. Each ring is one of the five Platonic solids, in the colour the Kaleidohedra app gives that solid.

Book Eleven. A Brief Geometry of Space

Opening

About 1645 the swordsman Miyamoto Musashi set down his way in five books: Earth, Water, Fire, Wind and the Void. Nineteen centuries before him Euclid had set down geometry as definitions, postulates and propositions, and ended by building the five regular solids and proving that there are no more. This work borrows from both. Its five rings are the five solids of DICTO’s name, each in the Kaleidohedra app’s colour for that solid: the cube for Earth, the icosahedron for Water, the tetrahedron for Fire, the octahedron for Wind, the dodecahedron for the Void. Earth lays out the pieces of space. Water follows them as they flow into one another. Fire is the meeting: two neighbours change places, and a cell of living skin is carried into four dimensions. Wind goes back to the old schools and their five solids. The Void goes beyond them, and comes home. One question runs through all five: what stays in place when the parts of space change places?

For a newcomer

One book from each ring, then the rest, with the DICTOnary at hand throughout:

  1. Book One, Quanta of Space I, the essay (Earth)
  2. Book Two, Space-Fillers and Lattice Moves (Earth)
  3. Book Six, HexOct: The Hexagonality Law (Fire)
  4. Book Seven, The Permutohedron Paradigm (Fire)
  5. Book Eight, The Platonic Solids (Wind)
  6. Book Eleven, A Brief Geometry of Space (Void)

A reader who knows the five solids may start at Book Eight. The Water books and Books Nine and Ten are taken when the main road points to them. The full order is the numbering.

Models: Miyamoto Musashi, Go Rin no Sho (The Book of Five Rings, 五輪書, about 1645); Euclid, Elements (about 300 BC), Book XIII for the five solids. Colours as in DICTO’s square, the Kaleidohedra app’s colours of the five solids.
